Critical Dynamics of Non-Reciprocally Coupled Conserved Systems
Emir Sezik, Gunnar Pruessner

Abstract
Non-reciprocal systems have been shown to sustain time-dependent patterns, most prominently travelling waves. The transition into these time-dependent states generally breaks time-translational invariance, representing a clear deviation from equilibrium dynamics. Though common implementations of non-reciprocity lead to such phenomenology, these spatio-temporal patterns are absent in other models. In the same vein, the ensuing scaling behaviour also depends on the precise way non-reciprocity is implemented. To better understand the effects of different non-reciprocal interactions, we study the critical conserved dynamics of non-reciprocally coupled spin systems. Specifically, we consider the dynamics of two n n -component order parameter fields ϕ i \bm{\phi}_{i} with i ∈ { 1 , 2 } i\in\{1,2\} . Unlike the common implementations of non-reciprocal interactions, we introduce the non-reciprocity solely through the non-linear interaction between the distinct species. Using the field-theoretic renormalisation group (RG) procedure, we perform a one-loop analysis and show that at one-loop level, the critical behaviour depends on the microscopic value of certain quantities. Using the flow functions, we elucidate the behaviour of the fixed points for different bare microscopic values. We also show that for n ≥ 4 n\geq 4 , there is a fixed point where the ensuing critical dynamics asymptotically obey detailed-balance, implying the emergent dynamics are agnostic to the microscopic non-reciprocity on large scales. Finally, we show that the conserved dynamics reduces the number of independent scaling exponents, mimicking the effect of a standard fluctuation-dissipation relation.



I
Introduction

Recently, non-reciprocal interactions have attracted much interest from the active matter community for its relevance in out-of-equilibrium systems
16
;
45
;
38
;
44
;
40
. They have been shown to possess an intriguing phenomenology ranging from synchronisation
34
;
35
to the formation of ”time crystals”
11
. Broadly, non-reciprocity refers to the notion that two entities interact in a way that breaks detailed balance (or Newton’s Third Law). Such interactions can even arise microscopically
6
;
7
;
45
in the context of social interactions (i.e. predator-prey systems)
33
or solids with odd elasticities
39
. Even though there are a myriad ways of implementing non-reciprocity in different contexts
17
;
26
;
10
;
4
;
27
;
36
;
3
;
37
, most implementations lead to the same physics: dynamical steady states with travelling patterns, most prominently waves
6
;
7
;
45
;
37
;
3
;
18
;
31
;
24
that break time-translational invariance. However, this is not the only phenomenology contained in non-reciprocal interactions. Other implementations
46
;
47
do not lead to spatio-temporal patterns, but to generic condensation behaviour in late times.

In a similar vein, the ensuing scaling behaviour also depends on the precise way the non-reciprocity is implemented. For systems where the non-reciprocity induces “run-and-chase” dynamics between species (where the species are coupled at least at the linear level), the transition into these states have been shown to be
effectively thermal

34
;
35
;
42
;
41
with most of the scaling exponents, except a novel one, being that of equilibrium Model A (or Model B if the order parameters are conserved) of Halperin’s classification
19
. This is no longer true, however, when the non-reciprocity enters only through the non-linear coupling between the species
46
;
47
. Here, it was argued that a true non-equilibrium fixed point emerges with strong dynamical scaling, where both fields display critical long-ranged fluctuations, with a distinct universality class. These varied results, seemingly in contradiction with universality, stem from the different underlying symmetries of the respective models. Different non-reciprocal interactions encompass different symmetries which in turn cause different critical behaviour. The systems in Refs.
42
;
37
;
24
;
30
, which include the most common implementations of non-reciprocal interactions, exhibit rotational symmetry characterized by the
U(1)\sim SO(2)
group, whereas the system in Ref.
46
possesses only
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, a far more restrictive symmetry. Therefore, exploring how different types of non-reciprocal interactions and symmetries affect the scaling behaviour of the system during the transition remains an intriguing research avenue.

Motivated by this, we investigate the critical dynamics of two conserved order parameter fields
\bm{\phi}_{i}
where
i\in\{1,2\}
with
\mathcal{O}(n)\otimes\mathcal{O}(n)
symmetry. We couple the spins non-reciprocally only at the non-linear level, in order to understand the behaviour of such couplings. By performing a perturbative renormalisation group (RG) calculation about the upper critical dimension, we show that to one loop, the fixed points depend on bare parameters of the model. This implies that to unveil the full universality class of the model, one needs to perform a
full

2-
loop analysis where every parameter in the model receives a non-trivial correction. A similar issue persists in the models considered in Refs.
46
;
47
. However, the authors of Refs.
46
;
47
only perform a partial
2-
loop analysis and we argue below that their results depend on bare parameters as well. We compute the scaling exponents of this model at this new critical point and show that for
n<4
, the universality class is distinct from equilibrium Model B. Furthermore, we show that even though the system is out of equilibrium, the conserved dynamics ensures the anomalous exponents of correlation and response function to be the same, mimicking the effect of standard fluctuation-dissipation relations.

The rest of the paper is organised as follows. In Sec.
II
we introduce the model, identify the critical points at the mean-field level and derive the field theory as well as the elements of the perturbative expansion. In Sec.
III
, we introduce the central objects of field-theoretic renormalisation, perform the renormalisation group calculation and use these results to calculate the flow functions. These are then used to explore the stable fixed points of the RG flow in Section
III.4
. We calculate the ensuing scaling exponents at these fixed points in Sec.
III.5
. Finally, we summarise and conclude in Sec.
IV
.

II
Setup

In this section, we introduce our model, its associated field theory, and the elements of the perturbative expansion.

II.1
Model

We are interested in the conserved relaxational dynamics of two local order parameter vector fields
\bm{\phi}_{1},\bm{\phi}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
in
d
-dimensional space
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, given by the Langevin equations

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\phi}}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{2}\frac{\delta\mathcal{H}{}_{1}}{\delta\bm{\phi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)}+\frac{g_{12}}{2}\nabla^{2}\Big(\bm{\phi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)\bm{\phi}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)\cdot\bm{\phi}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)\Big)+\sqrt{2\Gamma_{1}}\bm{\xi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)

(1a)

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\phi}}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{2}\frac{\delta\mathcal{H}{}_{2}}{\delta\bm{\phi}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)}+\frac{g_{21}}{2}\nabla^{2}\Big(\bm{\phi}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)\bm{\phi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)\cdot\bm{\phi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)\Big)+\sqrt{2\Gamma_{2}}\bm{\xi}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)

(1b)

where the noises
\bm{\xi}_{1},\bm{\xi}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
are identically and independently distributed Gaussian random variables satisfying

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\xi}_{i}(\mathbf{x},t)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=0

(2a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\bm{\xi}_{i}(\mathbf{x},t)\bm{\xi}^{\intercal}_{j}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\mathds{1}_{n}\delta_{ij}\nabla^{2}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime})\ ,

(2b)

with
\mathds{1}_{n}
the
n\times n
identity.
In the following, we will use subscript
i,j\in\{1,2\}
to index species and, if needed,
superscript latin indices
a,b,c,d\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
to index components of vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, such as
\bm{\phi}_{1}=(\phi_{1}^{1},\phi_{1}^{2},\ldots,\phi_{1}^{n})^{\intercal}
. In this notation, the noise correlator is written
\left\langle\xi^{a}_{i}(\mathbf{x},t)\xi^{b}_{j}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right\rangle=-\delta_{ij}\delta^{ab}\nabla^{2}\delta(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime})
. Throughout, we will make use of the Einstein summation convention, where repeated indices are implicitly being summed over, in summations involving field components
a,b
.

Eqs. (
1
)
corresponds to the conserved dynamics of the non-reciprocally coupled spins studied in
46
;
47
. The right-hand sides of
Eqs. (
1
)
comprise three terms: Firstly, a single-species Hamiltonian, secondly an interaction between fields
\bm{\phi}_{1},\bm{\phi}_{2}
and thirdly a conserved noise with strength
\Gamma_{i}
. By inspection of
Eqs. (
1
)
and (
2
), both order parameter fields are globally conserved. The Hamiltonians
\mathcal{H}{}_{1},\mathcal{H}{}_{2}
contain single-species terms and are defined as

\mathcal{H}{{}_{i}}=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}\left(\frac{r_{i}}{2}\bm{\phi}_{i}^{2}+\frac{D_{i}}{2}(\nabla\bm{\phi}_{i})^{2}+\frac{u_{i}}{4!}(\bm{\phi}_{i}\cdot\bm{\phi}_{i})^{2}\right)

(3)

with

(\nabla\bm{\phi}_{i})^{2}=\sum_{a=1}^{n}\sum_{\mu=1}^{d}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\mu}}\phi_{i}^{a}\right)^{2}

(4)

where we use the Greek index
\mu
for the
d
components of
\nabla
. The dot-product
(\bm{\phi}_{i}\cdot\bm{\phi}_{i})
multiplies two vectors in the “spin-space”
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and is henceforth reserved for projections in the spin-space. Computing the functional derivatives acting on the fields in
Eqs. (
1
)
, we arrive at the following Langevin equations:

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\phi}_{1}}=\nabla^{2}\left[r_{1}-D_{1}\nabla^{2}+\frac{u_{1}}{3!}\bm{\phi}_{1}\cdot\bm{\phi}_{1}+\frac{g_{12}}{2}\bm{\phi}_{2}\cdot\bm{\phi}_{2}\right]\bm{\phi}_{1}+\sqrt{2\Gamma_{1}}\bm{\xi}_{1}

(5a)

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\phi}_{2}}=\nabla^{2}\left[r_{2}-D_{2}\nabla^{2}+\frac{u_{2}}{3!}\bm{\phi}_{2}\cdot\bm{\phi}_{2}+\frac{g_{21}}{2}\bm{\phi}_{1}\cdot\bm{\phi}_{1}\right]\bm{\phi}_{2}+\sqrt{2\Gamma_{2}}\bm{\xi}_{2}

(5b)

In the Hamiltonians
Eq. (
3
)
the mass
r_{i}
and the non-linearity
u_{i}
quantify the harmonic and the quartic part of the local potential of the
\bm{\phi}_{i}
field. After Fourier-transforming,
u_{i}
will mediate the single species interaction between the modes
\mathbf{k}
of
\bm{\phi}_{i}
. The
D_{i}
characterise the diffusivity of the fields. Individually, these systems are stable only for
u_{i}>0
. Both Hamiltonians are isotropic as
\mathcal{H}{{}_{i}}
do not change under global rotations
O(n)
of
\bm{\phi}_{i}
. In fact,
\dot{\bm{\phi}}_{1}

in
Eq. (
1a
)
is invariant under rotations of
\bm{\phi}_{2}
, because
\bm{\phi}_{2}
enters into
\dot{\bm{\phi}}_{1}
only by magnitude. Similarly,
\dot{\bm{\phi}}_{2}
of
Eq. (
1b
)
is invariant under rotations of
\bm{\phi}_{1}
. Furthermore, the statistics of the pair of local order parameter fields
\bm{\phi}_{1},\bm{\phi}_{2}
is unchanged under global rotations
O(n)\otimes O(n)
.

For particular choices of the parameters,
Eqs. (
1
)
describe an equilibrium system
8
;
14
;
15
;
13
. The condition for equilibrium is most easily identified by considering the Boltzmann distribution
\mathchoice{e^{-\tilde{\mathcal{H}{}}}}{\operatorname{exp}(-\tilde{\mathcal{H}{}})}{\operatorname{exp}(-\tilde{\mathcal{H}{}})}{\operatorname{exp}(-\tilde{\mathcal{H}{}})}
such that

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\phi}}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{1}\nabla^{2}\frac{\delta\tilde{\mathcal{H}{}}}{\delta\bm{\phi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)}+\sqrt{2\Gamma_{1}}\bm{\xi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)

(6a)

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\phi}}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{2}\nabla^{2}\frac{\delta\tilde{\mathcal{H}{}}}{\delta\bm{\phi}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)}+\sqrt{2\Gamma_{2}}\bm{\xi}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)

(6b)

which implies

\tilde{\mathcal{H}{}}=\frac{\mathcal{H}{{}_{1}}}{\Gamma_{1}}+\frac{\mathcal{H}{{}_{2}}}{\Gamma_{2}}+\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}\frac{g_{12}}{4\Gamma_{1}}\bm{\phi}_{1}^{2}\bm{\phi}_{2}^{2}

(7)

using
Eq. (
3
)
,
Eqs. (
6
)
and (
7
) immediately reproduce
Eq. (
1a
)
, but
Eq. (
1b
)
if and only if
g_{12}\Gamma_{2}=g_{21}\Gamma_{1}
. This condition is trivially fulfilled if
g_{12}
and
g_{21}
both vanish, such that the two fields
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
do not interact. In that case, their dynamics corresponds to that of two
isolated
systems, possibly at different temperatures
\Gamma_{1,2}
. When
g_{12}
does not vanish, equilibrium is obtained only when the two systems have effectively, namely concerning their coupling, the same temperature
\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{1}g_{21}/g_{12}
. To capture the equilibrium condition more succinctly, we introduce the ratio
\sigma
, such that

\frac{g_{21}}{\Gamma_{2}}=\sigma\frac{g_{12}}{\Gamma_{1}}

(8)

47
introduced a re-parameterisation that renders
g_{21}/g_{12}\in\{-1,0,1\}
, making
\sigma
a ratio of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
or simply
0
, but the terms parameterised by
g_{12}
and
g_{21}
in
Eq. (
1
)
generally acquire different corrections under renormalisation, breaking effectively
g_{21}/g_{12}\in\{-1,0,1\}
beyond the tree level.

We expect qualitatively different behaviour depending on the signs of
g_{21}
and
g_{12}
. If they have the same sign, then the phenomenology might not be too far from equilibrium. If one coupling vanishes, the corresponding field displays standard Model B behaviour, while the other is still subject to its fluctuations. However, if they have opposite signs, then one field reduces and one field increases the mass of the other field. Such a “truly non-reciprocal setup” is not commonly studied in the literature
37
;
32
;
24
;
31
;
45
;
7
;
16
, where the non-reciprocity enters linearly and thereby induces run-and-chase dynamics. The critical dynamics of such models has been analysed in
42
, where the effective dynamics are governed by a complex Landau-Ginzburg equation
18
. It was shown that the critical dynamics of that model is effectively thermal, a situation also seen in
29
;
34
;
35
.

II.2
Critical Points of the Model

In this section, we perform a mean-field analysis to unveil the phase diagram of the model. Typically, a mean-field analysis consists of finding stable homogenous configurations
\bm{\phi}_{i}(\mathbf{x},t)=\bm{\varphi}_{i}
that solve
Eqs. (
5
)
in the limit of vanishing noise. However, due to the
\nabla^{2}
prefactor,
any
homogeneous configuration will trivially solve
Eqs. (
5
)
in the zero-noise limit.

Furthermore, due to the conserved nature of the dynamics, the total field

\bm{\Phi}_{i}(t)=\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}\bm{\phi}_{i}(\mathbf{x},t),

(9)

is set by the initialisation and does not change in time,
which readily follows from integrating
Eqs. (
5
)
over all space. In the following mean-field analysis we choose
\bm{\Phi}_{i}=\mathbf{0}
, physically corresponding to an initial configuration with zero overall “magnetisation” at all times. Any spontaneous symmetry breaking will need to happen locally: the field will “phase separate”, whereby different regions of space will have different magnetisation direction joined together by possibly broad “interfaces” while the total global magnetisation stays at
\bm{\Phi}_{i}=\mathbf{0}
.

We will thus consider in the following a groundstate of the form

\bm{\phi}_{i}(\mathbf{x},t)=\varphi_{i}\bm{d}_{i}(\mathbf{x}),

(10)

where
\varphi_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{0,+}
is the global magnitude of the magnetisation and
\bm{d}_{i}(\mathbf{x})
is the director, a unit vector characterising the spatial modulation of the magnetisation.
This director
\bm{d}_{i}(\mathbf{x})
must satisfy

\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}\bm{d}_{i}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{0},

(11)

to ensure that the total magnetisation
\bm{\Phi}_{i}
vanishes.

The nature of the configuration, essentially defined by the director, is dependent on the number of field components
n
. For
n>1
, the system can sustain spin waves, which have minimal energetic cost. However, for
n=1
, such a smooth “interface” is no longer possible and local phases are separated by a sharp boundaries, also known as a domain walla. In this case,
d_{i}(\mathbf{x})
may be the sign of one component of
\mathbf{x}
, say
d_{i}(\mathbf{x})=\text{sgn}(x^{1})
which satisfies
Eq. (
11
)
. Though the nature of the stable configurations for
n=1
seems different to that of
n>1
, which is what we will focus on in the following, the subsequent mean-field analysis performed will hold true for all
n\geq 1
.

The phases of the model, and consequently the critical behaviour upon crossing different phases, is controlled by the values of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
.
To characterise the stable ground states of the model
Eqs. (
1
)
, we consider solutions of the form
Eq. (
10
)
.
Specifically, for a finite system of length
L
and volume
V=L^{d}
, they are given by spin waves with the smallest wave-vector
|\mathbf{q}|=2\pi/L
, satisfying
\nabla^{2}\bm{d}_{i}(\mathbf{x})=-(2\pi/L)^{2}\bm{d}_{i}(\mathbf{x})
. Inserting this into
Eqs. (
5
)
and using the unit vector property, we find that the
\varphi_{i}
satisfy:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2\pi}{L}\right)^{2}\left[r_{1}+D_{1}\left(\frac{2\pi}{L}\right)^{2}+\frac{u_{1}}{6}\varphi_{1}^{2}+\frac{g_{12}}{2}\varphi_{2}^{2}\right]\varphi_{1}\bm{d}_{1}(\mathbf{x})=0

(12a)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2\pi}{L}\right)^{2}\left[r_{2}+D_{2}\left(\frac{2\pi}{L}\right)^{2}+\frac{u_{2}}{6}\varphi_{2}^{2}+\frac{g_{21}}{2}\varphi_{1}^{2}\right]\varphi_{2}\bm{d}_{2}(\mathbf{x})=0

(12b)

We see that the existence of the spatial variation of the director adds an energetic cost that scales like
D_{i}/L^{2}
, but vanishes in the thermodynamics limit,
L\to\infty
. We are interested in the values of
\varphi_{i}
in the thermodynamic limit. The analysis simplifies if we assume
u_{i}>0
, which is justified as otherwise the local potential allows for configurations where at least one of the
\varphi_{i}
diverges. There is no such constraint on
g_{12}
and
g_{21}
. Defining
\tilde{\varphi}_{i}=\sqrt{u_{i}/6}\varphi_{i}
, as well as
g_{1}=3g_{12}/u_{2}
and
g_{2}=3g_{21}/u_{1}
,
Eq. (
12
)
gives in the thermodynamic limit

\displaystyle\left(r_{1}+\tilde{\varphi}_{1}^{2}+g_{1}\varphi_{2}^{2}\right)\tilde{\varphi}_{1}

\displaystyle=0

(13a)

\displaystyle\left(r_{2}+\tilde{\varphi}_{2}^{2}+g_{2}\varphi_{1}^{2}\right)\tilde{\varphi}_{2}

\displaystyle=0\ .

(13b)

Solving
Eqs. (
13
)
yields several possible configurations for the fields. However, not all of them are viable as they are unstable against small perturbations. To find the stable solutions, we perform a linear stability analysis whereby we perturb the solutions by a small amount,
\tilde{\varphi}_{i}\to\tilde{\varphi}_{i}+\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{i}
, and analyse the resulting linear force. Plugging this perturbation in
Eqs. (
13
)
and retaining terms to linear order in
\delta\tilde{\varphi}_{i}
, we find that the stability of the solutions are governed by the matrix

\mathcal{M}=\begin{pmatrix}r_{1}+3\tilde{\varphi}_{1}^{2}+g_{1}\tilde{\varphi}^{2}_{2}&2g_{1}\tilde{\varphi}_{1}\tilde{\varphi}_{2}\\
2g_{2}\tilde{\varphi}_{1}\tilde{\varphi}_{2}&r_{2}+3\tilde{\varphi}_{2}^{2}+g_{2}\tilde{\varphi}^{2}_{1}\end{pmatrix}\ .

(14)

For the solutions to be stable, we require the matrix
\mathcal{M}
to have only positive eigenvalues.

The
\tilde{\varphi}_{i}
solving
Eqs. (
13
)
can be found easily. There are four cases in total, depending on how many and which of the
\tilde{\varphi}_{i}
vanish,

(I):

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{\varphi}_{1}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{\varphi}_{2}=0

(15a)

(II):

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{\varphi}_{1}=\pm\sqrt{-r_{1}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{\varphi}_{2}=0

(15b)

(III):

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{\varphi}_{1}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{\varphi}_{2}=\pm\sqrt{-r_{2}}

(15c)

(IV):

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{\varphi}_{1}=\pm\sqrt{\frac{g_{1}r_{2}-r_{1}}{1-g_{1}g_{2}}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{\varphi}_{2}=\pm\sqrt{\frac{g_{2}r_{1}-r_{2}}{1-g_{1}g_{2}}}

(15d)

Without recourse to the stability, we can immediately deduce that cases (II) and (III) require negative masses
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, respectively. Similarly, the final case (IV) requires
(g_{1}r_{2}-r_{1})/(1-g_{1}g_{2})
and
(g_{2}r_{1}-r_{2})/(1-g_{1}g_{2})
to be simultaneously positive. To determine the viable solutions for a given
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, we compute the eigenvalues of the matrix
\mathcal{M}
associated with each cases and determine the regions, parametrised by
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, where each case yields positive eigenvalues for the matrix
\mathcal{M}
. After some straightforward algebra, we find:

(\tilde{\varphi}_{1},\tilde{\varphi}_{2})=\begin{cases}\text{(I)},\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ r_{1}>0\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ r_{2}>0\\
\text{(II)},\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ r_{1}<0\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ r_{2}>g_{2}r_{1}\\
\text{(III)},\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ r_{1}>g_{1}r_{2}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ r_{2}<0\\
\text{(IV)},\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ r_{1}<g_{1}r_{2}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ r_{2}<g_{2}r_{1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ 1-g_{1}g_{2}>0\ .\end{cases}

(16)

If both masses are positive,
r_{1},r_{2}>0
, the trivial solution (I) is stable, whereas cases (II) and (III) require at least one negative
r_{i}
. Though not immediately obvious, the stability condition of case (IV) cannot be met when
r_{1},r_{2}>0
. Therefore, non-trivial solutions start appearing when at least one of the masses
r_{i}
is negative.
Eq. (
16
)
also suggests that the topology of the phase diagram depend on the values of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
. Indeed, there are
6
cases to consider, depending on the sign of each
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
and
1-g_{1}g_{2}
, as illustrated in Figure
1
. We find that if the fields are coupled non-reciprocally,
i.e.

g_{1}g_{2}<0
, the phase defined by case (IV) always exists, as opposed to equilibrium, where its existence is confined to particular values of
g_{1}g_{2}
.

Figure 1:
The mean-field phase diagram of the model for different values of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
where the Roman numerals correspond to the phases defined by
Eqs. (
15
)
. The qualitative features of the phase diagram depend on the conditions satisfied by
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
,
Eqs. (
16
)
. Phase (IV) of both fields non-vanishing, exists ony when for
g_{1}g_{2}<1
.

The phase diagrams in Figure
1
each display four phase boundaries (two of which coalesce to one when
g_{1}g_{2}=1
, Figure
1
b), with locations that depend on the precise values of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
, all meeting at the tricritical point
r_{1}=r_{2}=0
. Therefore, we expect there to be
5
overall continuous phase transitions, namely the traversal of each phase boundary and the tricritical point. Except for the tricritical point, at each of the phase boundaries, exactly one field displays ordering dynamics in the presence of another field (ordered or not), exerting additional, finite-ranged noise. For all these transitions, the ordering dynamics is covered by the equilibrium Model B behaviour of Halperin and Hohenberg’s classification
20
. This is easily seen for
r_{1}\to 0
and
r_{2}>0
or vice versa.
The dynamics near this critical point is described by

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\phi}_{1}}(\mathbf{x},t)=\nabla^{2}\left[r_{1}-D_{1}\nabla^{2}+\frac{u_{1}}{3!}\bm{\phi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)\cdot\bm{\phi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)\right]\bm{\phi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)+\sqrt{2\Gamma_{1}}\bm{\xi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)

(17a)

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)=\mathbf{0}\ .

(17b)

At this phase boundary, the short and long-ranged field decouple and the latter undergoes equilibrium Model B dynamics. This scenario also holds at the phase boundaries where one of the fields, say
\bm{\phi}_{2}
, has already ordered,
r_{2}<0
, as in case (III),
Eq. (
16
)
. In such a regime, the
\mathcal{O}(n)
symmetry associated with the ordered field is spontaneously broken and the effective theory is given for the Goldstone modes whose direction lie on the the
n
-dimensional sphere. On sufficiently large scales,
we may therefore assume constant
\bm{\phi}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)\cdot\bm{\phi}_{2}(\mathbf{x},t)
.
We can then replace any instance of
|\bm{\phi}_{2}|^{2}
in the equation of motion of
\bm{\phi}_{1}
by the mean-field value
|\bm{\phi}_{2}|^{2}=-3g_{12}r_{2}/u_{2}=-g_{1}r_{2}
, yielding

\dot{\bm{\phi}_{1}}(\mathbf{x},t)=\nabla^{2}\left[\left(r_{1}-r_{2}g_{1}\right)-D_{1}\nabla^{2}+\frac{u_{1}}{3!}\bm{\phi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)\cdot\bm{\phi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)\right]\bm{\phi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)+\sqrt{2\Gamma_{1}}\bm{\xi}_{1}(\mathbf{x},t)

(18)

which displays standard Model B behaviour when
r_{1}
is tuned to its critical value,
r_{1,c}\equiv r_{2}g_{1}
.

The only phase transition that is not covered by Model B is therefore the tricritical point where
both
fields undergo ordering dynamics and display non-Gaussian fluctuations.
In what follows, we use renormalised field theory with minimal subtraction and dimensional regularisation with
d=d_{\rm c}-\epsilon

43
;
48
;
25
;
2
to characterise it. To identify the upper critical dimension,
d_{\rm c}=4
, of this model, we perform the usual power counting
43
.
Choosing
[x]=L
to be the unit of length, we demand fluctuations and noise remain invariant under spatial rescaling,
i.e.

[\Gamma_{i}]=B_{i}
and
[D_{i}]=A_{i}
,
Eqs. (
1
)
and (
2
), so that the unit of time becomes
[t]=L^{4}/A_{i}
, which means that
A_{1}=A_{2}=A
. The fields then have dimensions
[\bm{\phi}_{i}(\mathbf{x},t)]=L^{\frac{2-d}{2}}B_{i}^{1/2}A^{-1/2}
and thus
[u_{1}]=[g_{21}]=L^{d-4}A^{2}B_{1}^{-1}
and
[u_{2}]=[g_{12}]=L^{d-4}A^{2}B_{2}^{-1}
. For
d>d_{\rm c}=4
, these couplings become irrelevant and the critical theory is described by the mean field exponents. Otherwise, they become relevant.

II.3
Field Theory

In this sub-section, we derive the field theory and elucidate its structure, following the structure long established in the literature
22
;
5
;
23
;
21
;
43
;
28
;
12
.
The action
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{0}+\mathcal{A}_{I}
can be separated into the harmonic part

\mathcal{A}_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int d^{d}x\int dt\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}\cdot\left[\left(\partial_{t}-\nabla^{2}(r_{i}-D_{i}\nabla^{2})\right)\bm{\phi}_{i}+\Gamma_{i}\nabla^{2}\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}\right]

(19)

and the perturbative part

\mathcal{A}_{I}=-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{u_{i}}{3!}\int d^{d}x\int dt(\bm{\phi}_{i}\cdot\bm{\phi}_{i})(\bm{\phi}_{i}\cdot\nabla^{2}\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i})-\int d^{d}x\int dt\left[\frac{g_{12}}{2}(\bm{\phi}_{2}\cdot\bm{\phi}_{2})(\bm{\phi}_{1}\cdot\nabla^{2}\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{1})+\frac{g_{21}}{2}(\bm{\phi}_{1}\cdot\bm{\phi}_{1})(\bm{\phi}_{2}\cdot\nabla^{2}\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{2})\right]

(20)

where the
\nabla^{2}
of the Langevin
Eq. (
1
)
has been moved to act (only) on the auxiliary fields
\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}
by the use of Gauss’ theorem and the action enters into the path integral in the form
\mathchoice{e^{-\mathcal{A}}}{\operatorname{exp}(-\mathcal{A})}{\operatorname{exp}(-\mathcal{A})}{\operatorname{exp}(-\mathcal{A})}
. To diagonalise the harmonic part of the action, we Fourier transform the fields

\displaystyle\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}(\mathbf{x},t)=\int\text{\dj}^{d}k\int\text{\dj}\omega e^{\mathring{\imath}(\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}-\omega t)}\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

(21a)

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}_{i}(\mathbf{x},t)=\int\text{\dj}^{d}k\int\text{\dj}\omega e^{\mathring{\imath}(\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}-\omega t)}\bm{\phi}_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

(21b)

where
\text{\dj}^{d}k=d^{d}k/(2\pi)^{d}
and
\text{\dj}\omega=d\omega/(2\pi)
. Correspondingly we use
\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k})\vcentcolon=(2\pi)^{d}\delta(\mathbf{k})
and
\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega)\vcentcolon=2\pi\delta(\omega)
.
The harmonic part of the action thus becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int\text{\dj}^{d}k\int\text{\dj}\omega\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}(-\mathbf{k},-\omega)\cdot\left[\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathbf{k}^{2}(r_{i}+D_{i}\mathbf{k}^{2})\right)\bm{\phi}_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)-\Gamma_{i}\mathbf{k}^{2}\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\right]

(22a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int\text{\dj}^{d}k\int\text{\dj}\omega\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}(-\mathbf{k},-\omega)&\bm{\phi}_{i}(-\mathbf{k},-\omega)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-2\Gamma_{i}\mathbf{k}^{2}&-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{k})\\
\mathring{\imath}\omega+\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{k})&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\\
\bm{\phi}_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\end{pmatrix}

(22b)

where we have introduced
\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{k})\vcentcolon=\mathbf{k}^{2}(r_{i}+D_{i}\mathbf{k}^{2})
. Inverting the matrix in
Eq. (
22b
)
, we can find the bare propagators and the noise vertices

(23b)

where the Kronecker
\delta^{ab}
is a consequence of rotational invariance. Although
Eqs. (
22
)
are the bare propagators, proportionality to
\delta^{ab}
of the propagators holds to all orders, i.e. different components never “mix”. This is in fact an exact result. Here and in the following shortened legs indicate amputated diagrams, such as the two noise vertices in
Eqs. (
23b
)
. The dash on the emerging lines of these diagrams denote the multiplication by the
\mathbf{k}
carried by them, corresponding to a gradient in real space.

C_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\nu)\delta^{ab}\equiv\langle\phi_{i}^{a}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\phi_{i}^{b}(\mathbf{q},\nu)\rangle
can be built from the noise vertices by attaching two propagators. At bare level, they are given by

To find the interaction vertices, we rewrite the perturbative part of the action
Eq. (
20
)
in terms of the Fourier transformed fields and we take the opportunity to render the summation over the components of the fields symmetric, say

\int\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}_{1,2,3,4}\,\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}_{1,2,3,4}\ \delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}\tilde{\phi}^{a}(\mathbf{k}_{1},\omega_{1})\phi^{b}(\mathbf{k}_{2},\omega_{2})\phi^{c}(\mathbf{k}_{3},\omega_{3})\phi^{d}(\mathbf{k}_{4},\omega_{4})\\
=\int\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}_{1,2,3,4}\,\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}_{1,2,3,4}\ \frac{1}{3}\big(\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}+\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc}\big)\tilde{\phi}^{a}(\mathbf{k}_{1},\omega_{1})\phi^{b}(\mathbf{k}_{2},\omega_{2})\phi^{c}(\mathbf{k}_{3},\omega_{3})\phi^{d}(\mathbf{k}_{4},\omega_{4})

(25)

using the Einstein convention. The perturbative part of the action then becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{I}

\displaystyle=\int\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}k\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q^{\prime}\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q^{\prime\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q^{\prime\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q^{\prime\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}^{d}q^{\prime\prime}\,}\int\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}{\!\text{\dj}\omega\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}\nu\,}{\!\text{\dj}\nu\,}{\!\text{\dj}\nu\,}{\!\text{\dj}\nu\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}\nu^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}\nu^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}\nu^{\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}\nu^{\prime}\,}\mathchoice{\!\text{\dj}\nu^{\prime\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}\nu^{\prime\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}\nu^{\prime\prime}\,}{\!\text{\dj}\nu^{\prime\prime}\,}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\left(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{q}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime}\right)\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\left(\omega+\nu+\nu^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime\prime}\right)

(26)

\displaystyle\ \times\mathbf{k}^{2}\Bigg[\frac{u_{1}}{3\times 3!}Q^{abcd}\tilde{\phi}^{a}_{1}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\phi^{b}_{1}(\mathbf{q},\nu)\phi^{c}_{1}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\phi^{d}_{1}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime})+\frac{u_{2}}{3\times 3!}Q^{abcd}\tilde{\phi}^{a}_{2}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\phi^{b}_{2}(\mathbf{q},\nu)\phi^{c}_{2}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\phi^{d}_{2}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad\ +\frac{g_{12}}{2}\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}\tilde{\phi}^{a}_{1}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\phi^{b}_{1}(\mathbf{q},\nu)\phi^{c}_{2}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\phi^{d}_{2}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime})+\frac{g_{21}}{2}\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}\tilde{\phi}^{a}_{2}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\phi^{b}_{2}(\mathbf{q},\nu)\phi^{c}_{1}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\phi^{d}_{1}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime})\Bigg]

where
Q^{abcd}\vcentcolon=\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}+\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc}
.

We read off the interaction vertices from
Eq. (
26
)
,

(27b)

III
Renormalisation Group Procedure

In this section, we carry out the Renormalisation Group (RG) programme for the field theory introduced in the previous section. We focus on the tricritical point,
r_{1}\to 0
and
r_{2}\to 0
simultaneously, where both fields display non-Gaussian fluctuations.

III.1
Renormalisation Scheme

In field theoretic RG, the aim is to regularise the logarithmic UV-divergences that appear in the perturbative expansion, most easily implemented via dimensional regularisation. Upon regularising the perturbative expansion and through the use of the Callan-Symanzik equation (see Section
III.5
) these divergences determine the critical behaviour, in particular the critical exponents of the theory.

This regularisation is done most efficiently by considering
vertex functions
associated with various correlation functions
43
;
2
;
25
. These vertex functions are, firstly the two-point vertices

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta^{ab}}{G_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)},

(28a)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\tilde{\phi}_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=\delta^{ab}\frac{C_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)}{G_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)G_{i}(-\mathbf{k},-\omega)}

(28b)

namely the inverse propagator for
i\in\{1,2\}
and the noise vertex, again for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Secondly, the
four-point vertices

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\phi_{j}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k},\omega;\mathbf{q},\nu,\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\left(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{q}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime}\right)\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\left(\omega+\nu+\nu^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime\prime}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\phi_{i}^{a}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\tilde{\phi}_{i}^{b}(\mathbf{q},\nu)\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{c}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\tilde{\phi}_{j}^{d}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime})\rangle_{c}}{G_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)G_{i}(-\mathbf{q},-\nu)G_{j}(-\mathbf{q}^{\prime},-\nu^{\prime})G_{j}(-\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},-\nu^{\prime\prime})},

(28c)

which the effective non-linearities,
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, corresponding to
u_{i}
for
i=j
and
g_{ij}
for
i\neq j
.
In the definitions
Eqs. (
28
)

G_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)
and
C_{i}(\mathbf{k},\omega)
are the full propagators and correlation functions, respectively. The subscript “c” denotes the connected diagrams. At tree-level, the vertex functions,
Eqs. (
28
)
, can be read off from the action,
Eqs. (
19
)
and (
20
):

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=\delta^{ab}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathbf{k}^{2}(r_{i}+D_{i}\mathbf{k}^{2})\right)+\mathcal{O}(\lambda),

(29a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\tilde{\phi}_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=2\Gamma_{i}\mathbf{k}^{2}\delta^{ab}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda),

(29b)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k},\omega;\mathbf{q},\nu,\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{k}^{2}\frac{u_{i}}{3}Q^{abcd}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),

(29c)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\phi_{j}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k},\omega;\mathbf{q},\nu,\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{k}^{2}g_{ij}\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),

(29d)

where
i\neq j
in the final line,
\lambda\in\{u_{1},u_{2},g_{12},g_{21}\}
denotes the non-linear coupling constants of the theory, so that
\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})
in
Eqs. (
29
)
contains only loops, which will be accounted as explained in the following.

During the RG procedure, higher order vertices or higher order frequency and wave-vector dependence in the vertices in Eq. (
29
) will be generated. However, these have no bearing on the critical behaviour of the theory as they are RG
irrelevant
, according to their engineering dimensions, Section
II.2
. Thus, in what follows, we consider the corrections by loop diagrams to the bare parameters in
Eqs. (
29
)
. Generally, the RG relevant or marginal parts of the vertex functions can be written as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=\delta^{ab}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega\mathcal{I}_{\omega_{i}}+\mathbf{k}^{2}(\mathcal{I}_{\tau_{i}}\tau_{i}+\mathcal{I}_{D_{i}}D_{i}\mathbf{k}^{2})\right),

(30a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\tilde{\phi}_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=2\Gamma_{i}\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{i}}\mathbf{k}^{2}\delta^{ab},

(30b)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{k}^{2}\frac{u_{i}}{3}\mathcal{I}_{u_{i}}Q^{abcd},

(30c)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\phi_{j}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{k}^{2}g_{ij}\mathcal{I}_{g_{ij}}\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd},

(30d)

where once again
i\neq j
. In
Eqs. (
30
)
we have introduced the loop corrections
\mathcal{I}_{p}
with
p\in\{\omega_{i},D_{i},r_{i},\Gamma_{i},u_{i},g_{12},g_{21}\}
, which will be evaluated at
\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0}
and
\omega=0
. The mass of the inverse propagator,
Eq. (
30a
)
, is now rewritten as
\tau_{i}=r_{i}-r_{i,c}
.

We have further defined

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\tilde{\phi}_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\tilde{\phi}_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},0),

(31a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k},0;-\mathbf{k}/3,0,-\mathbf{k}/3,0,-\mathbf{k}/3,0),

(31b)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\phi_{j}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\phi_{j}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k},0;-\mathbf{k}/3,0,-\mathbf{k}/3,0,-\mathbf{k}/3,0),

(31c)

for notational ease. The choice of
\mathbf{k}/3
on the right-hand side of
Eqs. (
31
)
is not the subtraction point
25
, as we will take
\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0}
below, but rather a convenient choice to define the effective renormalised couplings below.

To perform the RG procedure, we first identify the tricritical point of the theory where the correlation lengths of both fields diverge. At mean-field level, this tricritical point is

r_{1,c}=r_{2,c}=0
.
However, as is well understood
43
, this value will get shifted due to the fluctuations of the fields. The critical point
(r_{1,c},r_{2,c})
is determined by the simultaneous roots of:

\left.\frac{\partial\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{1}\phi_{1}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)}{\partial\mathbf{k}^{2}}\right|_{\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0},\omega=0}=\left.\frac{\partial\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{2}\phi_{2}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)}{\partial\mathbf{k}^{2}}\right|_{\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0},\omega=0}=0

(32)

This ensures that the fluctuations of the fields are massless and hence have infinite correlation length. Upon identifying the critical point of the model, we can parameterise the system in terms of
\tau_{1}=r_{1}-r_{1,c}
and
\tau_{2}=r-r_{2,c}
which measure the distance from the tricritical point. We can now proceed with eliminating the divergences of our theory. This elimination is done at an arbitrary inverse length scale
\mu
. To absorb the UV-divergences that arise in the perturbative expansion, we introduce the following
Z
-factors and renormalised quantities, indicated by the subscript
R
: Firstly the field renormalisation,

\bm{\phi}_{i,R}=Z_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}\bm{\phi}_{i},\hskip 5.69046pt\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i,R}=Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i},

(33)

and secondly the renormalisation of the couplings,

\displaystyle\tau_{i,R}=Z_{\tau_{i}}\tau_{i}\mu^{-2},D_{i,R}=Z_{D_{i}}D_{i},

(34a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i,R}=Z_{\Gamma_{i}}\Gamma_{i},

(34b)

\displaystyle u_{i,R}=Z_{u_{i}}u_{i}\mu^{\epsilon},\hskip 5.69046pt

(34c)

\displaystyle g_{ij,R}=Z_{g_{ij}}g_{ij}\mu^{\epsilon}\ .

(34d)

Any renormalised coupling has been made spatially dimensionless using
\epsilon=4-d
for the non-linearities. With this adjustment, renormalised couplings remain finite and any physical observable will be given in terms of the renormalised quantities which are the well-defined objects in the field theory.

We choose the normalisation point (NP) of the renormalisation scheme at a finite
\tau_{1,R}
and
\tau_{2,R}
, approaching the tricritical point as
\mu\to 0
. Under renormalisation, the ratio
t=\tau_{1}/\tau_{2}
might flow away from its bare value, which we will need to characterise in principle, however, it turns out that
t
enters solely into itself.

Furthermore, we impose, adhering to the minimal subtraction scheme
25
, the following normalisation conditions on the renormalised vertices to leading order in the divergence in
1/\epsilon
:

\displaystyle\left.\Gamma_{R,\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}}^{ab}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)\right.|_{\rm NP}

\displaystyle=\delta^{ab}\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+\mathbf{k}^{2}(\mu^{2}\tau_{i,R}+D_{i,R}\mathbf{k}^{2})\right),

(35a)

\displaystyle\left.\Gamma_{R,\tilde{\phi}_{i}\tilde{\phi}_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k})\right.|_{\rm NP}

\displaystyle=2\Gamma_{i,R}\mathbf{k}^{2}\delta^{ab},

(35b)

\displaystyle\left.\Gamma_{R,\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})\right.|_{\rm NP}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{k}^{2}\frac{u_{i,R}\mu^{\epsilon}}{3}Q^{abcd}

(35c)

\displaystyle\left.\Gamma_{R,\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\phi_{j}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})\right.|_{\rm NP}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{k}^{2}g_{ij,R}\mu^{\epsilon}\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd},

(35d)

where
i\neq j
in the last line. The renormalised vertices may be written in terms of the renormalised fields as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{R,\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}}^{ab}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta^{ab}}{G_{i,R}(\mathbf{k},\omega)},

(36a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{R,\tilde{\phi}_{i}\tilde{\phi}_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=\delta^{ab}\frac{C_{i,R}(\mathbf{k},\omega)}{|G_{i,R}(\mathbf{k},\omega)|^{2}},

(36b)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{R,\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\phi_{j}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k},\omega;\mathbf{q},\nu,\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\left(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{q}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime}\right)\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22\left(\omega+\nu+\nu^{\prime}+\nu^{\prime\prime}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\phi_{i,R}^{a}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\tilde{\phi}_{i,R}^{b}(\mathbf{q},\nu)\tilde{\phi}_{j,R}^{c}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\tilde{\phi}_{j,R}^{d}(\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},\nu^{\prime\prime})\rangle_{c}}{G_{i,R}(\mathbf{k},\omega)G_{i,R}(-\mathbf{q},-\nu)G_{j,R}(-\mathbf{q}^{\prime},-\nu^{\prime})G_{j,R}(-\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime},-\nu^{\prime\prime})}.

(36c)

where
\langle\phi_{i,R}^{a}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\tilde{\phi}_{i,R}^{b}(\mathbf{q},\nu)\rangle=\delta^{ab}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\nu)G_{i,R}(\mathbf{k},\omega)
and
\langle\phi_{i,R}^{a}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\phi_{i,R}^{b}(\mathbf{q},\nu)\rangle=\delta^{ab}\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})\delta\mkern-6.0mu\mathchar 22(\omega+\nu)C_{i,R}(\mathbf{k},\omega)
.
Using the definition of the renormalised fields,
Eq. (
33
)
in
Eqs. (
35
)
and comparing to
Eq. (
28
)
, it follows that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{R,\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}}^{ab}\left(\mathbf{k},\omega\right)

\displaystyle=\left(Z_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}\right)^{-1}\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega),

(37a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{R,\tilde{\phi}_{i}\tilde{\phi}_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)

\displaystyle=Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}^{-2}\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\tilde{\phi}_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega),

(37b)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{R,\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}^{-1}Z_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}^{-3}\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k}),

(37c)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{R,\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\phi_{j}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}^{-1}Z_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}^{-1}Z_{\bm{\phi}_{j}}^{-2}\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{j}\phi_{j}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})

(37d)

where
i\neq j
in the last line. Using
Eqs. (
37
)
in the normalisation condition
Eq. (
35
)
and comparing to the parameterisation
Eqs. (
30
)
allows us to express
the loop-corrections
\mathcal{I}_{p}
in terms of
the
Z
-factors,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\omega_{i}}

\displaystyle=Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}Z_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}

(38a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\tau_{i}}

\displaystyle=Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}Z_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}Z_{\tau_{i}}

(38b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{D_{i}}

\displaystyle=Z_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}Z_{D_{i}}

(38c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{i}}

\displaystyle=Z^{2}_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}Z_{\Gamma_{i}}

(38d)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{u_{i}}

\displaystyle=Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}Z^{3}_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}Z_{u_{i}}

(38e)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{g_{ij}}

\displaystyle=Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}Z_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}Z^{2}_{\bm{\phi}_{j}}Z_{g_{ij}}

(38f)

and thus conversely the
Z
-factors, which are of the form
Z=1+\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
, in terms of the loop corrections
\mathcal{I}_{p}
of the same form. We determine them in Section
III.3
, after deriving some exact results, in particular
Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{1}}Z_{\bm{\phi}_{1}}=Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{2}}Z_{\bm{\phi}_{2}}=1
,
Eq. (
41
)
, which significantly simplifies
Eqs. (
38
)
.

III.2
Ward Identities

In this section, we derive Ward Identities, which amount to exact results about some
Z-
factors. The most obvious symmetry is the conserved nature of the dynamics, as every deterministic term in
Eqs. (
1
)
is preceded by a
\nabla^{2}
and the noise is conserved. Perturbatively, this results in corrections to the propagator, or any other correlation function for that matter, to vanish at
\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0}
. Non-perturbatively, the integral over all space of the propagator is unity,

\int\mathchoice{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}{\!\mathrm{d}^{d}x\,}\left\langle\phi^{a}_{i}(\mathbf{x},t)\tilde{\phi}^{b}_{j}(\mathbf{0},t^{\prime})\right\rangle=\theta(t-t^{\prime})\delta_{ij}\delta^{ab}\ .

(39)

At the level of the field theory, this imposes Ward identities which, in turn, yield certain relations among the
Z
-factors, most easily identified by writing
Eq. (
39
)
as

\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0},\omega)=-\mathring{\imath}\omega\delta^{ab}\ ,

(40)

which correspondingly has a unit amplitude
43
.
Evaluating
Eq. (
40
)
with
Eqs. (
35a
)
and (
37a
) produces

Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{1}}Z_{\bm{\phi}_{1}}=Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{2}}Z_{\bm{\phi}_{2}}=1,

(41)

so that
\mathcal{I}_{\omega_{i}}=1
,
Eq. (
38a
)
.
Corrections to the conserved noise vertex acquire at least a factor
\mathbf{k}^{4}
, so that

\left.\frac{\partial\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\tilde{\phi}_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)}{\partial\mathbf{k}^{2}}\right|_{\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{0},\omega=0}=-2\Gamma_{i}\delta^{ab}

(42)

to all orders and therefore with
Eqs. (
35b
)
and (
37b
)

Z_{\Gamma_{i}}Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}^{2}=1\ ,

(43)

so that
\mathcal{I}_{\Gamma_{i}}=1
,
Eq. (
38d
)
.
Together with
Eq. (
41
)
it follows that

Z_{\Gamma_{i}}=Z_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}/Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}

(44)

which in equilibrium is obtained from the Ward identity following from a fluctuation-dissipation relation
43
. In the present case, however, we have a distinctly non-equilibrium system with no apparent fluctuation-dissipation relation. Yet, the conserved dynamics gives sufficient constraints to produce the same effect
Eq. (
44
)
of a standard fluctuation-dissipation relation. Furthermore, we show in Section
III.5
that
Eqs. (
41
)
and (
43
) are sufficient to ensure that the anomalous exponents of the correlation and response functions are the same. Of course, this does not imply that the present system sustains a “hidden” fluctuation-dissipation relation.

III.3
1-Loop Renormalisation

In this section, we calculate the
Z
-factors
Eqs. (
34
)
imposing
Eqs. (
35
)
, or, equivalently, by calculating the loop corrections and reading off from
Eqs. (
30
)
the
\mathcal{I}_{p}
, which generate the
Z
-factors by
Eqs. (
38
)
. The actual evaluation of the loop diagrams is relegated to Appendix
A
. Here, we merely quote the results.

Starting with the renormalisation of the inverse propagators,
\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)
, to one loop

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})

(45b)

where the first set of terms is the bare theory and the minus sign is due to Dyson-summing one-loop diagrams. Rather than performing an additive renormalisation
43
, we replace
r_{i}
by
\tau_{i}=\mu^{2}\tau_{i,R}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
,
Eq. (
34a
)
, regularising the infrared (IR). Where we encounter IR divergences of the form
\tau_{i}^{-\epsilon/2}
, we may replace them by
\mu^{-\epsilon/2}
for both
i=1
and
i=2
to leading order in small
\epsilon
as
\tau_{i,R}/D_{i,R}
are finite. A ratio of the form
\tau_{2}/\tau_{1}
, however, is
\tau_{1,R}/\tau_{2,R}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
. As for the ultraviolet (UV), we use the analytic continuation of the UV-divergence captured by
\Gamma(1-d/2)=-\Gamma(\epsilon/2)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})
to write
Eq. (
45
)
as

\displaystyle\left.\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{1}\phi_{1}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\right|_{NP}

\displaystyle=\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+D_{1}\mathbf{k}^{4}+\frac{\mu^{2}\tau_{1,R}}{Z_{\tau_{1}}}\mathbf{k}^{2}\left[1-\frac{n+2}{6}\frac{u_{1}\Gamma_{1}}{D_{1}^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}-\frac{n}{2}\frac{g_{12}\Gamma_{2}}{D_{2}^{2}}\frac{\tau_{2}}{\tau_{1}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}\right]\right)\delta^{ab}

(46a)

\displaystyle\left.\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{2}\phi_{2}}^{ab}(\mathbf{k},\omega)\right|_{NP}

\displaystyle=\left(-\mathring{\imath}\omega+D_{2}\mathbf{k}^{4}+\frac{\mu^{2}\tau_{2,R}}{Z_{\tau_{2}}}\mathbf{k}^{2}\left[1-\frac{n+2}{6}\frac{u_{2}\Gamma_{2}}{D_{2}^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}-\frac{n}{2}\frac{g_{21}\Gamma_{1}}{D_{1}^{2}}\frac{\tau_{1}}{\tau_{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}\right]\right)\delta^{ab}

(46b)

where

\epsilon=4-d
.
As
Z_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}=1
,
Eq. (
41
)
, the loop integrals
\mathcal{I}_{\tau_{i}}
equal the
Z
-factors
Z_{\tau_{i}}
,
Eqs. (
38b
)
and (
38c
), and are readily identified as the square-brackets in
Eqs. (
46
)
, so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\tau_{1}}

\displaystyle=Z_{\tau_{1}}=1-\left(\frac{n+2}{6}\frac{u_{1}\Gamma_{1}}{D_{1}^{2}}+\frac{n}{2}\frac{g_{12}\Gamma_{2}}{D_{2}^{2}}\frac{\tau_{2}}{\tau_{1}}\right)\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}

(47a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\tau_{2}}

\displaystyle=Z_{\tau_{2}}=1-\left(\frac{n+2}{6}\frac{u_{2}\Gamma_{2}}{D_{2}^{2}}+\frac{n}{2}\frac{g_{21}\Gamma_{1}}{D_{1}^{2}}\frac{\tau_{1}}{\tau_{2}}\right)\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}

(47b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{D_{1}}

\displaystyle=Z_{D_{1}}=1

(47c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{D_{2}}

\displaystyle=Z_{D_{2}}=1\ .

(47d)

We now consider the renormalisation of the single species quartic vertices
\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})
. To one loop, the (amputated) diagrams are given by

Using
Eqs. (
95
)
-(
97
), the vertex at the normalisation point to one loop order takes the form

\displaystyle\left.\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{1}\phi_{1}\phi_{1}\phi_{1}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})\right|_{NP}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{k}^{2}\frac{Q^{abcd}}{3}u_{1}\left[1-u_{1}\frac{n+8}{6}\frac{\Gamma_{1}}{D_{1}^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}-\frac{g_{12}g_{21}}{u_{1}}\frac{3n}{2}\frac{\Gamma_{2}}{D_{2}^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}\right],

(49a)

\displaystyle\left.\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{2}\phi_{2}\phi_{2}\phi_{2}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})\right|_{NP}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{k}^{2}\frac{Q^{abcd}}{3}u_{2}\left[1-u_{2}\frac{n+8}{6}\frac{\Gamma_{2}}{D_{2}^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}-\frac{g_{12}g_{21}}{u_{2}}\frac{3n}{2}\frac{\Gamma_{1}}{D_{1}^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}\right]\ ,

(49b)

which allows us to read off
\mathcal{I}_{u_{i}}
as of
Eqs. (
35c
)
and (
38e
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{u_{1}}

\displaystyle=Z_{\Gamma_{1}}Z_{u_{1}}=1-\left(u_{1}\frac{n+8}{6}\frac{\Gamma_{1}}{D_{1}^{2}}+\frac{g_{12}g_{21}}{u_{1}}\frac{3n}{2}\frac{\Gamma_{2}}{D_{2}^{2}}\right)\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}

(50a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{u_{2}}

\displaystyle=Z_{\Gamma_{2}}Z_{u_{2}}=1-\left(u_{2}\frac{n+8}{6}\frac{\Gamma_{2}}{D_{2}^{2}}+\frac{g_{12}g_{21}}{u_{2}}\frac{3n}{2}\frac{\Gamma_{1}}{D_{1}^{2}}\right)\frac{\Gamma\left(\epsilon/2\right)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\mu^{-\epsilon}

(50b)

using that
Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}Z^{3}_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}=Z^{-2}_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}=Z_{\Gamma_{i}}
as
Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}Z_{\bm{\phi}_{i}}=1
,
Eq. (
41
)
, and
Z_{\bm{\tilde{\phi}}_{i}}^{2}=1/Z_{\Gamma_{i}}
,
Eq. (
43
)
.

We finally consider the renormalisation of the cross-species quartic vertices
\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}\phi_{i}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})
. To one loop, the (amputated) diagrams are given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\tilde{\phi}_{1}\phi_{1}\phi_{2}\phi_{2}}^{abcd}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\hbox to52.88pt{\vbox to54.58pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 26.46208pt\lower-27.29135pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-23.12907pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{72}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
